### Interventions in improving flood and water resources management to expand Pakistan's regulations and policies  
National, subnational and community leaders and representatives will be trained to use the modified procedures. This work will allow the Government of Pakistan to invest in EbA and green infrastructure solutions and replicate similar adaptation measures in other flood- and drought-prone areas of Pakistan.  

### Component 2: Enhanced national and subnational governance and capacity  
15. Component 2 comprises the following outputs:  
- (a) Output 2.1: Evidence base to support the adoption of EbA and green infrastructure interventions by the Government of Pakistan;  
- (b) Output 2.2: Updated procedures for implementing EbA and green infrastructure in Pakistan; and  
- (c) Output 2.3: Strengthened capacity for implementing EbA and green infrastructure interventions in the Indus Basin for flood risk and water resources management.  

### Component 3: Enhanced community resilience and adoption of EbA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan’s Indus Basin (total cost: USD 3.2 million; GCF cost: USD 2.1 million)  
16. Component 3 will increase the resilience of community livelihoods in the Indus Basin by training communities and smallholder farmers on how to improve the existing agricultural practices that are prevalent throughout the Indus Basin to better withstand current and future climate change scenarios through improved irrigation practices to conserve water, improved soil management to increase yields and reduce agricultural expansion, and the introduction of new crop varieties that require less water. In addition, new opportunities for sustainable enterprises will be supported to provide alternative revenue-generating opportunities for communities in the Indus Basin and to reduce ecosystem degradation caused by traditional livelihoods that are directly dependent on natural resources. The procurement of project pipelines, the incubation of environmentally sustainable businesses and the provision of technical assistance will help to reduce ecosystem degradation and increase revenue generation for businesses and families.  

17. Component 3 will be achieved through the two key outputs:  
- (a) Output 3.1: Pipeline of feasible climate-resilient businesses; and  
- (b) Output 3.2: Improved climate-resilient livelihoods among vulnerable target communities.

## 20. The project will directly benefit 687,336 people, of which 48 per cent are female. The number of indirect beneficiaries is estimated to reach 7,024,361 people, which accounts for 3.5 per cent of the total population of the country.

## 21. The direct benefits will be as follows:  

### (a) EbA interventions:  
reduction of flood impacts on populations close to the intervention sites (within 3 km) and for people living downstream;

### (b) Green infrastructure interventions:  
localized slowing of flood waters and the subsequent temporary storage and infiltration of water outside of flooding events;

### (c) Community-based adaptation interventions:  
increased climate resilience of 50,750 of the most vulnerable smallholder farmers in the project sites.

## 22. The EbA interventions will provide mitigation co-benefits from afforestation, reforestation and assisted natural regeneration of a reduction in emissions of 26,450 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO2 eq) per year, with the total lifetime removals estimated at 1,037,928 t CO2 eq.

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential  
Scale: Medium to high  

## 23. The project creates a paradigm shift in Pakistan’s flood and water resources management efforts by establishing proofs of concept for EbA and green infrastructure interventions in the Indus Basin that validate the cost-efficiency, benefits and sustainability of these adaptation and resilience measures. The project will implement watershed basin-wide EbA interventions to manage flood risk, as a complement to grey infrastructure, and mainstreaming EbA into water, agriculture, natural resources management and disaster reduction management plans.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 172  

In the funding proposal, the model uses a social discount rate of 9.12 per cent, which has been computed as the average of the deposit interest rate in Pakistan in 2020 (7.5 per cent) and the rate of the country’s commercial banks (10.6 per cent). The rate used also follows the approved FAO project (FP108) in the GCF portfolio.  

In summary, the economic model results show the net present value as USD 30 million and the economic internal rate of return as 13.6 per cent, indicating that the proposed project is economically viable.  

### 35. Financial adequacy and the appropriateness of concessionality is assessed to be high.  

From the supply side, banks and financial institutions in the target areas of Pakistan are currently not providing loans to smallholder farmers owing to the risks associated with uncertainty in profitability. From the demand side, smallholder farmers (around 63–85 per cent of farmers in Pakistan) have limited financial literacy, collateral and credit history, and thus very limited access to finance in general.  

To address this issue, a strong business case is needed to ease financial risks, and the proposal is requesting grant funding from GCF to showcase such a business case. The justification is deemed satisfactory.  

### 36. The need for grants in this project is also justified by the public nature of project activities.  

In summary, 95.9 per cent of the project costs are for public goods (e.g., rehabilitation of degraded lands for flood risk reduction). For output 3.1 (pipeline development for climate-resilient businesses), GCF resources are to be used for technical assistance and capacity-building for potential businesses. For output 3.2 (climate-resilient agriculture), 84 per cent of funding is for technical assistance and training.  

The remaining USD 222,600 is for climate-smart agriculture inputs and water saving schemes (irrigation and water storage tanks) but the size of the funding was argued to be too small to conduct a formal financial model.  

### 37. The cost-effectiveness of the project is assessed to be medium.  

The project’s adaptation cost is USD 111/direct beneficiary and USD 10/total beneficiary. Cost per direct beneficiary is in the high range of the GCF portfolio for climate resilience in agriculture, compared with the other GCF-approved projects in the portfolio in Pakistan.  

Although the project is targeting adaptation results areas only, the AE estimated mitigation co-benefits from carbon sequestration through forest afforestation, reforestation and assisted natural regeneration.  

If the total GCF funding amount is regarded as mitigation cost, the cost per t CO2 eq would then be USD 73.22/t CO2 eq over the 40-year lifetime, which is within the price range of USD 40–80/t CO2 eq to meet the Paris Agreement target (World Bank, 2021).  

### 38. The co-financing ratio is 1:0.18, which is lower than the target under the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2020–2023.  

The AE has provided justification for the low co-financing, particularly as substantial government budget, once planned as cofinance, has been redirected towards emergency relief because of the climate-related disasters and subsequent economic impacts in the country.  

The proposal does not describe the potential to leverage or mobilize additional financial resources, but the AE has indicated that it will implement mobilization strategies to ensure the sustainability of the project and the possible scale-up of the project to different areas, if successful.

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 173  

Based Adaptation (EbA) in Pakistan. The project will restore 14,215 ha of degraded watersheds and 34 km of flow paths and channels as EbA interventions. Furthermore, it will deploy 127 green infrastructure interventions tailored for specific locations in four project sites in the Indus Basin. The project is expected to have positive impacts on people and the environment. It will improve both the quality and quantity of water for irrigation and drinking purposes, augment water governance system, enhance flora and fauna, and facilitate the adoption of smart agriculture techniques. Ultimately the enduring effects of the project will lead to reinforced climate resilience and overall food and water security in the arid and semi-arid regions of Pakistan.

### 40. Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instrument.  
The AE has classified the project medium environmental and social (E&S) risk, which is equivalent to Category B as per GCF’s environmental and social risk categorization and within the AE’s E&S risk accreditation level. Given that the project itself is an environmental and social enhancement project, the Secretariat agrees with this categorization while noting the challenges faced by the project related to conflict sensitivity in certain project sites and the changing security context in the region. The project design and built-in safeguards have brought the overall risk level to moderate, including to mitigate the project’s security risk through operationalizing a Security Plan. As the detailed activities and their exact locations are yet to be defined, the AE has prepared an environmental and social management framework (ESMF), which also includes a process framework (PF) to address restrictions on land use and access to resources, and an indigenous peoples planning framework (IPPF) to address requirements for activities that are located near or within indigenous peoples’ communities and territories. A stakeholder engagement plan (SEP) was prepared separately, which contains consultations conducted to date for the project design and outlines a detail process for further stakeholder engagement during project implementation.

### 41. Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguard (ESS) standards.  
The paragraphs below provide a brief description of the project’s compliance with ESS standards.

### 42. ESS1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts.  
The project is categorized as medium risk against the AE’s safeguards integrated policies and procedures. The ESMF provides general information about the profiles of the project areas, the baseline information about the project socio-economic conditions, the archetypes of project interventions and a related methodological framework for their further screening and planning at the site level. While the current baseline information is derived from socio-economic, ecological, and biophysical data collected for the project’s initial scoping, the project will conduct environmental scoping studies and develop thorough environmental baselines, including indicators for assessing the biophysical impacts of the proposed interventions, during the project implementation. The ESMF identifies the project’s E&S risks assessed against the project’s safeguards screening criteria per each project component, with the general mitigation measures and roles and responsibilities to bring the mitigation measures into practice specified. The ESMF provides the relevant tools and processes for further assessing the site-specific E&S risks and impacts, including the potential cumulative effects. If the screening reveals any adverse environmental or social impacts that may arise from the planned activity, an ESMP will be required before the initiation of the project activity and following the guidelines provided in the ESMF.

### 43. The ESMF includes a conflict and security assessment to delineate the project’s conflict and security related risks and potential impacts.  
Given the complex security situation in the region, the ESMF identifies that two project sites (Chakkar Lehri and DI Khan) overlay with the conflict areas, which are considered by the AE as medium security risk zones. The project site in Chakkar Lehri is situated in Balochistan, the largest and most underdeveloped region, which has undergone inter-tribal conflicts and conflicts between law enforcement agencies and the separatist movement. The assessment notes sporadic incidents of violence and attacks in DI Khan.

## Page 174  

Khan and that the changing security situation in Afghanistan may embolden terrorist factions in Pakistan. The AE has devised a Security Plan based on its institutional experience of working in conflict-prone settings and with local institutions and communities to establish credibility and install confidence.  

The Security Plan, as part of the ESMF, includes a set of mitigation measures, risk reduction techniques, and security protocols to be followed by the project to manage such conflict sensitivity, such as through the engagement with the security forces and law enforcement agencies, close collaboration with tribal elders in the targeted provinces to ensure the project’s legitimacy and seek their advice avoid becoming unintentionally part of any conflict.  

In the case of security threats, the project will pursue measures including immediate withdrawal from project areas where security threats are identified.  

In addition to the screening of conflict sensitivity risk that will take place at activity- or site-level during implementation, the AE is suggested to conduct the site-specific conflict sensitivity assessments to inform the detailed design, siting, and stakeholder engagements.  

The AE is also suggested to continuously monitor the effectiveness of the Security Plan to ensure robust safety and security arrangements are put in place. Where high security or other risks of the project arise, the implementation of the ESMF and the project should be responsive to the changing conditions and the results of monitoring throughout the project’s lifecycle.  

## 44. ESS2: Labour and working conditions  

While the ground activities are yet to be identified and specified during the project implementation, the project will require hiring and engagement of project staff, field workers and unskilled labour.  

The ESMF has identified potential risks of unsafe labour practices, inadequate occupational health and safety equipment, child labour, and workplace discrimination, pertaining to the construction works, such as building flood dispersal embankments, excavating flow paths, and desilting ponds to enhance the water-holding capacity, to be conducted as part of the EbA and green infrastructure interventions.  

Following the safeguard eligibility and impacts screening list to be applied at site-level, project activities involving child labour, forced labour, sexual exploitation, or other forms of exploitation, will be screened out as ineligible activities.  

The ESMF requires that the project implement all reasonable precautions to protect the health and safety of workers through the introduction of preventive and protective measures, and that all labour hired for the excavation work be provided with proper safety gear, temporary housing, first aid training and the relevant health and safety related insurance cover.  

The project will hire labour from locals of the area and ensure the labour rights of project-employed workers will be observed. Further screening on labour and working conditions will be conducted and detail mitigation measures will be addressed in the site-specific ESMPs.  

## 45. ESS3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention  

The ESMF identifies that the works involved in the EbA and green instruction solutions, such as excavation of silt, debris, and other obstructions and pest use in afforestation can cause pollution.  

The misplacement and improper disposal of excavated material could lead to polluting the landscape and degrading the natural habitat.  

The ESMF requires a waste management plan to be formulated as part of the site-specific ESMP before the commencement of the relevant activities.  

The project will use the silt from the excavation work to rehabilitate and reinforce the sides of the flow paths as part of the EbA solutions.  

In terms of potential pollution caused by pest use, the project will exclude activities involving the procurement and/or use of pesticides and other chemicals specified as persistent organic pollutants or hazardous chemicals. Although the project will not directly promote the procurement and use of pesticides, there are chances that the project interventions, such as riverine afforestation, re-vegetation, nursery raising, and the change in agricultural practices, could lead to the procurement and use of pesticides.  

The ESMF includes a pest management plan that includes the guiding principles and a five-step approach on integrated pest management to be followed during project implementation. The site-specific E&S screening and ESMPs will further identify and address the project’s potential environmental impacts.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 175  

### 46. ESS4: Community health, safety and security  
The project is expected to benefit the health and safety of the local communities by reducing the risk of water directly flooding their agricultural lands, assets, and infrastructure. However, local communities may be exposed to risks related to the construction works, which may affect water turbidity and water table levels and lead to potential reduction in fish catch. Some project activities may reduce the access to water for irrigation purposes, especially for communities living downstream of the Indus River. There is also the potential risk that the project may inadvertently exacerbate community conflicts due to the restriction of access to land or resources and due to the selection of project beneficiaries and the access to the project benefits.  

The project will mitigate such risks by preventing adverse impact on water quality and supply to affected communities; ensuring the proper design and safety of the project infrastructure; putting in place protective mechanisms for the use of hazardous materials; following disease prevention procedures; and implementing the project’s security plan to ensure emergency preparedness and response. To avoid escalating or creating community conflicts, the project will further screen the risks and potential impacts on community health and safety at the site-level during implementation. Additionally, following the process framework (PF) provided in the ESMF, affected communities will participate in the identification, design, implementation and monitoring of project activities and the related mitigation measures to prevent and minimize the project’s potential adverse impacts on communities.  

As the exact impacts of the project on community health, safety and security will be further identified at the site-level through the safeguards screening and during project implementation, the AE is suggested to ensure site-specific assessment and mitigation measures and the operation of the project’s PF and security plan will mutually reinforce one another.  

### 47. ESS5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement  
The project will exclude activities that lead to private land acquisition, physical displacement, and voluntary or involuntary relocation of people, including non-titled and migrant people. The ESMF identifies that the project activities may involve novel or rigorous restrictions on the use of or access to natural resources, and that there may be some small areas of land acquisition, and that certain interventions, such as those pertaining to cleaning up the lake, could lead to changes in tenure arrangements, community-based property rights or customary rights to land, territories, and/or resources.  

The AE has triggered its own standard on restriction of access and resettlement and developed a process framework (PF) to guarantee transparency and equity in the planning and implementation of project activities and to avoid and minimize the project’s adverse impacts on the resource-dependent local communities and their livelihoods. The PF outlines the processes of engaging the local communities in the design, implementation and monitoring of the project; delineating the prospective negative impacts of the restriction on the encompassing communities, including any gender differences; defining the eligibility criteria of the economically displaced communities to receive compensation benefits and development assistance; identifying mitigation measures to assist any economically displaced people to restore and improve livelihoods; and setting out the participatory monitoring arrangements and a grievance redress procedure to resolve possible disputes on restrictions on land use and access to resources pertaining to the project.  

The PF will work with the safeguards screening to identify the exact social impacts of the project and address such impacts through site-specific ESMPs or developing specific livelihood restoration plans (LRPs). The PF details the processes and mitigation measures required, including on how to calculate and allocate the compensation to the affected communities. In the case of insufficient budget for an LRP as agreed with local communities, the project will redesign the specific activity/activities so that a LRP will no longer be needed, or the impact is lessened in a manner that a new LRP can be agreed upon.  

### 48. ESS6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources  
The project is expected to produce significant conservation benefits while harnessing biodiversity and ecosystem services to build climate resilience. It will exclude activities that could negatively affect areas of critical natural habitats or any breeding ground of known rare/endangered species, and activities that result in the loss of biodiversity, alteration

## Page 176  

The ESMF identifies four types of activities considered by the project that can adversely affect biodiversity and the integrity of terrestrial or aquatic ecosystems, if not managed properly. That is, revegetation and reforestation may lead to a decrease in water availability downstream and exotic or non-native tree species being planted; the construction of retention areas or rain ponds may affect local biodiversity; while dredging can improve water quality in lakes and wetlands, to clean the sediment may lead to large disturbance to the lake/wetland and that temporarily increased sediment concentrations can disturb aquatic life; and terracing may lead to habitat conversion and soil degradation, and can have impact on local hydrology and limiting water availability downstream.  

Further screening will be conducted at the site-level to screen out activities that may use genetically modified organisms or modern biotechnologies or their products; involve the procurement and/or use of pesticides and other chemicals specified as persistent organic pollutants or hazardous substances; or develop forest plantations.  

The AE is suggested to identify the potential impacts on biodiversity and natural resources per each archetype of the potential interventions and ensure that caution must be exercised in the design, selection, and implementation of site-specific interventions. The analysis of the project invention should also be informed by the environmental baseline to be developed at each site and be continuously monitored.  

## 49. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples  

Through screening undertaken in the context of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the AE has identified that there are indigenous peoples within the project sites. These include Mohana or Mallah communities in Machar Lake Bagri in Chaker Lehri and adjacent areas and Kehal and Mores of Taunsa and DI Khan areas.  

The AE has prepared an indigenous peoples planning framework as part of the environmental and social management framework with the intention of preparing indigenous peoples plans through a free, prior and informed consent process in partnership with the relevant communities. The AE noted that engagement has already been undertaken in these areas during the development of the funding proposal and that at this stage there is broad support from tribal elders for participation in the project.  

The stakeholder engagement plan acknowledges the influence of tribal groups in the project areas and the need to engage with and seek the approval of tribal elders.  

## 50. While planned activities could restore livelihoods, including for the Mohana, descendants of the first inhabitants of the Indus Valley who live in one of the last remaining floating villages on Lake Manchar and are dependent on the lake for fishing, there is also the risk that project activities could result in restricted access of Mohana people to fishing. This will be determined once the project activity locations are finalized, which is expected to be in the first six months of project implementation.  

The indigenous peoples planning framework notes that as many as 450 Mohana families may be affected by the project activities. Tribal groups exert influence in the project areas and there have also been historical tribal conflicts, including over water resources, in DI Khan and Balochistan, although the funding proposal documentation notes that this is less prevalent now and that the tribal elders have assured the AE of their cooperation, including through letters of commitment.  

To mitigate these risks, especially to Mohana communities and overall, the AE will implement free, prior and informed consent processes, taking into consideration the participation of women and men, and not implement in areas that will present adverse impacts to the local communities. The approval of tribal elders will be sought through their jirgah system before the start of all project activities.  

## 51. In relation to local and indigenous knowledge systems, EbA should provide opportunities for the inclusion and promotion of local and indigenous knowledge.  

The bulk of the activities are currently dedicated to green infrastructure as opposed to promoting bottom-up knowledge exchange, although traditional knowledge of water management, cultural landscape systems such as rain-fed ponds, wells and the karez system, are acknowledged in addition to other traditional forms of water-sharing. The karez system in particular is an ancient form of water management that allows communal water-sharing.

53. ESS8: Cultural heritage. The project will exclude activities that can negatively impact areas with cultural, historical, or transcendent values for individuals and communities. While no significant impact on the cultural heritage is expected from the project, the pre-feasibility study identifies possible location of historic sites in project areas therefore the AE has triggered its own standard on cultural resources to safeguard the project’s potential impacts applicable to both physical and intangible cultural resources. The pre-feasibility study recommended the need for further information for the project site in DI Khan, where historical structures built on the Luni and Gomal River diversion point near to Kot Azam were completely damaged during the super floods in 2010. More information is needed as to the extent of the damage and whether they continue to hold cultural significance regardless of the damage. The project site in the Manchar-Hamal Watershed, Lake Manchar, is also an important historical area. Home to several archaeological sites, there are likely intangible cultural resources for the Mohana people who have innumerable tangible and intangible cultural ties to the lake. An initial baseline information has been collected based on community surveys and field visits. Given the specific activities and locations of the project’s activities are yet to be determined, the ESMF provides a set of potential mitigation measures on cultural heritage, including to: conduct further baseline study and impact assessment on cultural heritage, as part of the landscape-level E&S screening and the efforts of identifying the indigenous peoples’ cultural heritage and complying with the regulatory requirements; prevent the negative impacts of the projects at the design stage to ensure that the planned activities do not harm the physical or cultural heritage; and develop a cultural heritage management plan prior to the implementation of project activities to preserve both tangible and intangible cultural heritage.

It recognizes that the achievement of biodiversity conservation is vital, while promoting gender equality, and makes every effort to ensure that project activities implemented by WWF respect integrity and human rights and mitigate any risk that gives rise to discriminatory and exploitative gender inequalities. The guidance note on GBV and SEAH will assist project teams in identifying any risks of GBV and SEAH that may emerge in conservation projects. # GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 178  

### 55. Implementation arrangements  
The ESMF describes the management structure and the roles and responsibilities related to project implementation and safeguard implementation. While the AE is responsible for ensuring both the GCF and AE’s own policy requirements and standards are applied and met, WWF Pakistan, as the EE, will be responsible for the implementation of all project components and activities, including ensuring the implementation of and compliance with the ESMF.  

A Project Management Unit (PMU), comprised of safeguard-related senior management including an E&S safeguards manager and a gender manager, will supervise the implementation of the ESMF and other safeguard plans at the site/landscape levels. It will also be responsible for the implementation.  

The PMU will be accountable to a Project Steering Committee (PSC), which will be established to provide technical guidance to the PMU and ensure alignment among the PMU with national, provincial and project-site level entities. The PSC will oversee the project’s compliance with the safeguard commitments and provide recommendations on specific safeguard issues where needed.  

In addition to the E&S safeguards manager, the project will also deploy three safeguard staff at the site-level responsible for the on-the-ground implementation of the ESMF and related plans once they have been finalized.  

### 56. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure  
The project underwent extensive consultations with key stakeholders respectively during the project design phase and during the preparation of the ESMF. Workshops, stakeholder meetings, and field-level consultations with local stakeholders and community groups were organized, and complemented by site visits, field inspections, and focus group discussions.  

The consultations during the ESMF preparation focused on identifying the potential adverse impacts of the project with mitigation options as well as assessing the potential project benefits and how these can be enhanced in favour of the local communities. It is concluded by the AE that the project affected communities were supportive of the planned project activities.  

A SEP has been prepared which summarizes the process and results of the consultations conducted so far and provides a detailed plan for further stakeholder engagement during the project’s implementation phase, covering the project’s executing partners and stakeholders, including non-government stakeholders, to be further engaged at the national, provincial, and local levels.  

The ESMF requires that all affected communities and relevant stakeholders be informed about the ESMF requirements and commitments, and that the project’s safeguard documentation, including site-specific ESMPs, will be disclosed to all affected communities and the public following a disclosure framework specified in the ESMF.  

### 57. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM)  
The ESMF provides the principles for the development of an activity-level GRM that will be finalized with training provided to the project’s safeguard personnel within the first six months of the project implementation.  

The project-level GRM will be operationalized, in addition to and independently from the EE’s own internal GRM system, following a separate structure, process flow, and hierarchy as outlined in the ESMF, which will enable all complaints going to straight to the PMU and with a whistle-blower mechanism to allow project communities to raise complaints anonymously.  

In addition to the project specific GRM, a complainant can submit a grievance to the AE’s independent, third-party GRM as well as to the project complaints officer, who is fully independent from the project team, hosted by the AE.  

The project will provide information of the GRMs available to the project, including GCF’s Independent Redress Mechanism as an integral, into the community consultations. All the consultation sessions will be conducted in the local language of the area, and the local communities will also receive training on how to file and lodge complaints.  

In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 179  

### 4.2 Gender policy  

58. The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal, thereby complying with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.  

59. The status of women in Pakistan over the last three decades has been fluctuating and has seen many structural factors/changes over time. The existing numerous laws put in place provisions that will ensure women’s rights and protection, but the implementation of the same and lack of political will is still a challenge. Women’s participation in decision-making processes remains a challenge, and formal institutions, including private and public institutions, fall short of facilitating women’s access to financial resources, education, skills, health facilities, etc. Informal/community institutions, including religious spaces, define and reinforce child marriages and gender roles at the household and/or community level.  

The Global Database on Violence against Women shows that 24.5 per cent of women experienced lifetime physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence between 2017 and 2018. The impact patterns are also different for women and men. A shrinking natural resource base has led to migration and shifts in livelihoods and roles, and there is a need for alternative livelihoods.  

60. The gender action plan for the Recharge Pakistan project was developed on the basis of the results and recommendations from the gender assessment, outlining gender-specific actions against each activity that aim to ensure that key gender issues are adequately incorporated in the overall project design and that stand-alone gender activities are carried out.  

The gender specialist/manager based at the PMU will lead the implementation of the gender action plan and facilitate various capacity needs assessments and training at the onset of the project implementation phase.  

The AE will also coordinate (on other outputs) with the National Commission on the Status of Women, the Women Development Department, the Council on Gender Equality, vocational training institutions and government agencies and line departments at the national and subnational level working on gender equality and women’s empowerment.  

The gender-specific activities that will be implemented under outcome 1 include:  
(a) integrating gender dimensions into the integrated floods and drought management Tool;  
(b) procurement of plant saplings from nurseries raised by local women;  
(c) formation of women’s groups on natural resources, water, climate change and environment;  
(d) capacity-building of local women and men to understand what climate change is, building their leadership skills so that they will be able to actively engage in the CBO meetings; and  
(e) holding consultative meetings with CBO women’s groups on operation and maintenance (O&M) of green infrastructure.  

Under outcome 2 the AE will:  
(a) ensure that gender considerations are included in the resource management regulatory frameworks during the development of policy briefs on EbA interventions;  
(b) sensitize relevant government line departments on gender and women’s empowerment in EbA across their planning and policy processes; and  
(c) The AE will set up a Project Steering Committee and provincial oversight committees, which will facilitate mainstreaming gender in initiatives and policies that focus on improving natural resource management through EbA and similar programmes.  

Finally, under outcome 3, the AE will:  
(a) ensure that women and men have access to capacity-building workshops focused on bankable projects/green financing;  
(b) ensure that women and men are trained on climate-resilient agriculture practices covering both drought and flooding scenarios; and  
(c) ensure that gender issues are mainstreamed in the implementation of climate-resilient agriculture interventions, including through highly efficient irrigation systems, kitchen gardening, fruit orchards, fodder cultivation and water storage tanks;  
(d) build the technical capacity of local women on alternative livelihoods and entrepreneurship; and  
(e) implement gender-specific diversified income-generating activities including, but not limited to, fruit and vegetable processing units (packaging, labelling and post-harvest processing along with value addition), handicrafts, poultry farming and apiculture.

# 4.3.1. Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

61. GCF is requested to provide up to USD 66 million by way of a grant, which will finance green infrastructure interventions using an EbA approach at the four project sites for flood risk management. The AE is providing co-financing of USD 1.8 million in kind and the United States Agency for International Development and the Coca-Cola Foundation are providing USD 5 million each in the form of a grant. The total funding amount is USD 77.8 million. The government of Pakistan is unable to provide any co-financing for the project owing to the diversion of the initial co-financing commitment to the national recovery following the floods in 2022.

62. The AE is requesting a full grant support because EbA and green infrastructure are often not considered to be economically viable in view of the high transaction cost, and the market is skewed towards hard infrastructure without the consideration of wider benefits from EbA. The analysis of the external service provider (Moody’s) assessed that the recent floods have exacerbated Pakistan's liquidity and external credit weaknesses.

# 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

63. WWF-US will serve as AE for this project. It will provide the EE with the GCF proceeds and be responsible for providing guidance to and supporting the EE.

64. WWF-Pakistan, the EE, has been operating in the country for over 50 years. The EE has a track record of working with the Government of Pakistan in policy and regulatory design and review as well as implementing EbA projects in Pakistan. The AE assessed the EE’s capacity and concluded that the EE is capable of applying WWF-US and GCF standards and policies in the execution of the project.

# 4.3.3. Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

65. O&M risk: the project stated that the O&M risk beyond the project implementation can be addressed at the government and community level. The funding proposal sets out the responsibilities of the relevant government departments for the required O&M activity. This will be further elaborated, including financing arrangements and budgetary allocation, during the implementation period. Given the current budgetary constraints, the government’s capacity to finance O&M cost beyond the project implementation is uncertain and the current commitment assumed that there would still be interest from the relevant ministries/governments. At the community level, the relevant provincial departments and CBOs will establish agreements on continuous monitoring and management of the green infrastructure and ecosystem protection.

The AE mentioned that the CBOs will work with a water user group to explore potential options for fee structures for irrigation and drinking water which may support the O&M. Another GCF-financed project (FP108) in Pakistan is working on water tariff matters, which could serve as a basis for the CBOs/water user groups to develop fee structures.

66. Foreign exchange rate risk: the AE identified foreign exchange rate risk as one of the risks which could affect the financial performance of the project such as overspending or underspending of the budget. The country has experienced significant local currency fluctuation (from –30 to +10 per cent) in the past three years and its foreign exchange reserves have fallen to very low levels according to the information provided by the external service provider in March 2023. In the case of significant exchange gain or loss, the AE should immediately inform GCF, which will take appropriate action by mutual agreement with the AE within the Board-approved financing amount. Moreover, the project is financed by full grant without government budgetary support, which will be less influenced by any repayment obligation and fiscal situation of the government.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 181  

### 67. The Dutch Fund for Climate and Development (DFCD) support projects (pre-identified):  
GCF proceeds under component 3 will be used to support projects pre-identified by DFCD. Since the preparatory funding from DFCD is over, the GCF proceeds will fill up the gap to allow these projects to be bankable and find investors. Mobilizing funds to cover the capital expenditure will be the key for the actual investment to materialize on the ground. The funding proposal includes an indicator of the number of signed agreements to ensure this.  

### 68. Economic analysis and project viability:  
the AE carried out an economic analysis, which resulted in an economic internal rate of return of 13 per cent over 30 years. The analysis considers the benefits from the avoided damage, cost savings in agriculture and carbon sequestration. Carbon sequestration is the greatest benefit derived from EbA and green infrastructure. The analysis includes a sensitivity testing with changes of inputs in underlying assumptions such as the low and high shadow price of carbon. The assumptions and referenced data used for analysis are deemed to be robust and justify the request for the grant. As the analysis assumed that the cost of O&M will continue beyond the project implementation, the long-term sustainability and viability of the project will depend on the capacity of the government and local communities to mobilize the resources to cover the O&M cost.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01

The funding proposal is an adaptation project focusing on two results areas of the Integrated Results Management Framework: (i) most vulnerable people and communities and (ii) ecosystems and ecosystem services. Through key interventions in EbA, green infrastructure and community-based adaptation in selected watershed areas of Pakistan, the project aims to benefit a total of 7.7 million individuals, with 0.7 million direct and 7.0 million indirect beneficiaries' adaptation interventions.  

The FP has been found to use the templates and provide contents in line with the Integrated Results Management Framework requirements. The sections on Theory of Change and logical framework have been aligned with the suggested structure and format. A continuous feedback mechanism has enabled refinement of the elements of the logical framework in terms of identification of results areas, indicators, means of verification, targets, assumptions, etc. They are found to be adequate and are expected to allow smooth monitoring and reporting of project results. The information provided in different sections of the logical framework are clearly articulated; their proper implementation will help to yield adaptation mechanisms, tools and practices for the communities in the country.  

The assessment of adaptation benefits and beneficiaries is a strength of the funding proposal. Following the Secretariat’s guidance, a separate annex on a methodology for the estimation of adaptation beneficiaries have been prepared, which provides a description of vulnerability context, identification of adaptation benefits and related beneficiaries. The direct and indirect beneficiaries are distinguished under a sound assumption. The methodology has allowed a systematic estimation of adaptation beneficiaries, which will help in devising approaches to reach them during implementation, as well as monitoring and reporting on adaptation beneficiaries.  

Implementation timetable: the implementation timetable has been provided in the required structure and format. The milestones and deliverables that are envisaged at different stages of project implementation are adequately planned. Adherence to the proposed implementation timetable will facilitate smooth implementation of the project as well as timely delivery of the results at planned intervals.

# Page 186

## Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of  
### FP207  
#### Proposal name: Recharge Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to climate change through Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) and Green Infrastructure for integrated flood risk management  

**Accredited entity:** World Wildlife Fund, Inc. (WWF)  
**Country/(ies):** Pakistan  
**Project/programme size:** Medium  

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview  

1. The proposed project will take place in Pakistan, a country characterized by a diversity of ecosystems, climate zones and landscapes (approximately 60 per cent rough mountainous terrain and plateaus, and 40 per cent level plain), and a wide variety of natural resources. With an economy long based on agriculture, Pakistan is now considered a semi-industrialized country facing considerable challenges in balancing the competing objectives of socioeconomic development and environmental protection. The numbers of people living below the poverty line (2013 data) and malnourished (2018 data) are estimated at 29.5 per cent and 12.3 per cent, respectively. Most of Pakistan’s population, estimated at 225.1 million, live in the Indus River basin, an area highly exposed to severe floods in July and August.

2. The proposed project targets two catchments of the Indus River basin, with interventions at four sites, namely Dera Ismail (DI) Khan and Ramak located in the DI Khan catchment, and Chakar Lehri and Manchar located in the Chakar Lehri catchment. Situated respectively in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province, and the Balochistan and Sindh provinces, the DI Khan and Chakar Lehri catchments have been selected because their ecosystems and communities are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, and because there are opportunities for adaptation benefits through ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) and green infrastructure interventions. Agriculture is the main resource for the subsistence of local people in the selected sites.

3. The total budget required to implement the project over its 7-year duration is USD 77,848,396, including USD 66,000,005 in the form of a grant from the GCF. The remainder will consist of USD 10 million from United States Agency for International Development (USAID) and the Coca-Cola Foundation, as well as an in-kind contribution from WWF-Pakistan estimated at USD 1,848,391.

4. Pakistan’s vulnerability to climate change is largely due to its geographical location and socioeconomic conditions:  
(a) Pakistan is located in a semi-arid to mostly arid region prone to recurrent large-scale floods, earthquakes, droughts and landslides. Its economy, notably the agricultural and hydroelectric sectors, is based on the development of water resources from rivers and glacial melt, which are highly dependent on climatic conditions. In recent years, the country’s climate has changed, with rising temperatures and more variable rainfall, compromising not only the functioning of the above-mentioned economic sectors, but also the livelihoods of local communities.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 187  

Also causing considerable loss and damage to Pakistan society as a whole. The major floods of 2010 and 2022 and their impacts are the most recent examples. With the climate conditions anticipated for the country – particularly an average temperature increase of up to 3.7 °C by 2060 and up to 6 °C by 2090, a decrease in the volume of glaciers and snow cover, and especially an increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme and slow-onset events – the situation is set to worsen if nothing is done to sustainably address these adverse climatic conditions, including major floods and droughts;  

(b) The adaptive capacity of Pakistani stakeholders to adverse climatic conditions attributable to climate change is greatly compromised by a number of unfavourable socioeconomic situations. Indeed, the country faces relatively high levels of malnutrition and of people living below the poverty line, and contains communities and groups marginalized by economic status, location and political circumstances. In addition, complex institutional context of Pakistan makes it difficult to adopt sustainable solutions to the challenges facing the country.  

5. The proposal’s theory of change states that “If the interventions envisaged by the funding proposal (EbA and green infrastructure) are integrated into the country’s planning systems and into future adaptation actions, then the targeted communities will be less vulnerable to floods and droughts, and governments at different levels will have the planning tools in hand to support and operationalize the interventions because the main barriers, notably the lack of proofs of concept for EbA and green infrastructure as viable adaptation intervention in Pakistan, the inadequate technical capacity across government and communities for implementing EbA and green infrastructure intervention in Pakistan, and the limited access to finance among the most vulnerable communities and micro-, small- and medium size enterprises for climate-resilient, sustainable businesses, will have been removed.”  

6. The main objective of the proposed project is to address climate change-induced floods and droughts in a sustainable way, using an anticipatory approach that combines EbA, green and green-grey infrastructures. To achieve this objective, the proposed project is built around three main components:  

- Component 1: proofs of concept for EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions as efficient and effective solutions to flood and drought risk reduction in Pakistan;  
- Component 2: enabling a paradigm shift towards EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure in Pakistan; and  
- Component 3: enhanced community resilience and adoption of EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions in Pakistan’s Indus basin.  

These components are expected to deliver the following results:  

- Outcome 1: EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions with improved community-led management will reduce flood risk and enhance the climate resilience of the most vulnerable people in Pakistan’s Indus basin;  
- Outcome 2: the development of an enabling environment for climate action that has new procedures for implementing EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions for flood and water resources management in Pakistan; and  
- Outcome 3: increased resilience of community livelihoods in the Indus basin.  

7. The World Wildlife Fund, Inc. (WWF-US) will serve as the accredited entity (AE) for the project and WWF-Pakistan will be the executing entity, which will establish and host the project management unit within WWF-Pakistan’s office in the Federal Capital, Islamabad. Furthermore, there will be four provincial implementation units hosted within WWF-Pakistan’s regional offices located in the provincial capitals (Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Sindh and Balochistan), and site implementation units will be based at field offices in the following locations:  

(i) DI Khan Watershed, covering the DI Khan and Ramak Watershed project areas;  
(ii) Chakar Lehri Watershed; and  
(iii) Manchar Lake Watershed.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01

### 8.  
The project interventions will directly benefit an estimated total of 687,336 people, including 357,414 men (52 per cent of the total population of the region) and 329,922 women (48 per cent of the total population), as well as indirectly benefiting 7,024,361 people, including 3,652,668 men (52 per cent of total population) and 3,371,693 women (48 per cent of total population) in Pakistan’s Indus basin.  

### 9.  
By strengthening the adaptive capacity of institutions and stakeholders at different administrative levels, the interventions promoted by the project are likely to be implemented and consequently have positive impacts in the management of large-scale floods and droughts in the country. This will be achieved through interventions on the ground in the four project sites to redirect and contain the flow of water, in order to minimize downstream flash floods and to facilitate infiltration to replenish groundwater resources and help address droughts.  

These interventions include EbA interventions such as restoration, reconnection and/or rehabilitation of watersheds, floodplains and wetlands; and green (and green-grey) infrastructure interventions such as retention areas and recharge basins. Community-based adaptation interventions will support vulnerable smallholder farmers to adopt drought-resilient crop varieties, to increase their capacity for resilient and sustainable irrigation and soil management, and to enhance irrigation through new water storage tanks.  

### 10.  
The proposed project should also contribute to changes in the regulatory and procedural arsenal for flood and drought management in Pakistan, to include climate-resilient land and water resources management strategies (e.g. expanding flood and water resources regulations and policies to prioritize EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions). The implementation of the regulations and procedures developed under the project should contribute to ensuring that the proposed interventions are adopted and consequently contribute to positive impacts on the socio-ecological systems facing the socioeconomic development challenges posed by floods and droughts in the targeted regions.  

### 11.  
Adaptation impact potential is rated as Medium to High.  

#### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential              Scale: Medium to high  

### 12.  
The proposed project aims to bring about a change in approach to planning for the management of major floods and droughts in Pakistan. Building on the positive results already achieved through the implementation of the EbA and green infrastructure interventions in other regions of the world, it intends to foster an anticipatory and integrated approach to the management of the above-mentioned climate risks, combining both EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions.  

In addition, to facilitate this change in the approach of the Government of Pakistan and various actors at the departmental level, the proposed project envisages setting up a new coordination structure, the National Working Group. Chaired by the Ministry of Climate Change and composed of regulators within the Ministry of Water Resources and its executive department, the Federal Flood Commission, as well as the relevant provincial departments, this National Working Group will lead the development and adoption of the procedures integrating the interventions promoted by the project.  

### 13.  
The proposed project will create an enabling environment for the long-term adoption of flood and drought management interventions in Pakistan. Indeed, it will be able to provide evidence on the efficiency and effectiveness of EbA and green infrastructure interventions, and promote reforms in the planning process and national and provincial policies for large-scale flood and drought management in Pakistan. In addition, the project will contribute to building community capacity on the issue. All this is likely to encourage the adoption of the proposed interventions beyond the project’s duration.  

### 14.  
The proposed project has a high potential for scaling up and replication. It will serve as the key proof point in Pakistan that EbA and green infrastructure interventions are cost-

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 191  

Great vulnerability to these extreme, slow-onset climatic events, which are set to intensify with climate change. The aforementioned floods caused considerable material and human damage, forcing the country to devote its meagre resources to repairing the damage caused. For example, the Government of Pakistan is no longer in a position to provide the co-financing originally envisaged for the proposed project, as resources have been diverted to emergencies linked to the major floods of 2022.

22. Pakistan’s adaptation needs, as well as those related to loss and damage caused by adverse weather conditions induced by climate change, are enormous. They are estimated at several billion United States dollars. Although Pakistan, one of the first countries in Asia to create a Ministry of Climate Change, has funds in the national budget for adaptation actions, it is unable to meet the immense needs in this field because of the urgent actions required by other development initiatives. In addition, the country’s high level of indebtedness, its economic development challenges and the national emergency resulting from the impacts of climate change mean that it can only provide limited financial support for adaptation actions. As a result, the country is obliged to turn to international public funding sources such as the Green Climate Fund.

23. Although climate change information and adaptation responses such as EbA exist, Pakistan’s water resources sector is still unable to integrate them into its planning processes, making it even more vulnerable to floods and droughts. This lack of integration of climate change issues into water resources management is due in particular to a weak institutional capacity to plan and implement adaptation actions. The proposed project intends to address these shortcomings/gaps by implementing activities such as EbA, and green and green-grey infrastructure in the project sites, and by improving the enabling environment to scale up such activities elsewhere in the country.

24. Needs of the recipients is rated as High.  
1.6 Country ownership              Scale: High  

25. The proposed project contributes to a set of objectives and priorities defined in the National Climate Change Policy, the framework document for addressing the current and future climate change challenges facing Pakistan. Indeed, the entire proposed project contributes to the objective of pursuing sustained economic growth by appropriately addressing the challenge of climate change. More specifically, outputs 2.1 and 2.2 contribute to the objective of integrating climate policy with other interrelated national policies; outputs 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, 3.1 and 3.2 contribute to the objective of focusing on pro-poor sensitive adaptation; outputs 1.1, 1.2, 1.3 and 3.2 contribute to the objective of ensuring water, food and energy security for the country in the face of challenges posed by climate change; outputs 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, 2.1, 2.2, 2.3 and 3.2 contribute to the objective of minimizing the risks arising from the expected increase in frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as floods, droughts and tropical storms; outputs 2.1, 2.2 and 2.3 contribute to the objective of strengthening interministerial decision-making and coordination mechanisms on climate change; and output 3.1 contributes to the objective of facilitating effective use of the opportunities, particularly financial, available both nationally and internationally. The proposed project is also in line with the National Forest Policy, the National Wetlands Policy, the National Disaster Risk Management Framework, the National Disaster Risk Reduction Policy, and the updated nationally determined contribution.

### 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness              Scale: Medium to high  

29. On the basis of the results of the cost–benefit analysis carried out in the context of the proposed project, the package of interventions envisaged is cost-beneficial. The total project investment will generate a net present value of USD 30 million with a benefit-to-cost ratio of 1:1 and an economic internal rate of return of approximately 13.6 per cent.

30. The project has no government co-finance, as a result of the diversion of funds to relief efforts after the major floods of 2022, but the AE is able to make a small contribution and to secure an additional USD 10 million in co-financing from Coca Cola and USAID, bringing the GCF to co-financing ratio to 1:0.18.

31. To inform the design of EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions, the proposed project includes best practices from similar projects including an optimized set of adaptation approaches that are described as “EbA and green infrastructure” in the funding proposal, but which are not all narrowly “green”, and can be considered to include soft engineered or hybrid “green-grey” measures, such as flood protection embankments, dispersal embankments, gabion bunds, retention areas and recharge basins.

32. In addition to the information gathered during the stakeholder consultation process, the results of the cost–benefit analysis and the case studies (notably from China1 and Afghanistan2) greatly guided the design of the project’s interventions. Indeed, the approach used in the case of the China project served as a model for most of the green infrastructure and EbA interventions considered by the proposed project.

33. According to the Working Group II contribution to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Sixth Assessment Report, the “understanding of EbA has improved considerably but there is medium agreement regarding its benefits (Doswald et al., 2014; Nalau et al., 2018a)”3 and “limited evidence and low agreement on its economic efficiency and long-term sustainability”.

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

37. Based on the above explanations, iTAP endorses the funding proposal “Recharge Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to climate change through Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) and Green Infrastructure for integrated flood risk management”.  

38. The iTAP recommends the following improvements to the funding proposal:  
(a) The formulation, from the very first years of the proposed project, of a realistic and sustainable strategy and plan for mobilizing resources for actions integrating EbA, green and green-grey infrastructures;  
(b) The formulation, at the early stage of the project implementation, of a clear protocol for assessing the efficiency and effectiveness of EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions;  
(c) Making an additional effort to better take into account scientific and technical references in the design of the EbA, green and green-grey infrastructure interventions; and  
(d) Due to the risk of disease propagation associated with the establishment of retention ponds (resulting from the probability of standing water), it is important to carefully reassess these risks and to ensure that appropriate mitigation measures are put in place.  

39. Considering that the proposal has largely satisfied all the GCF’s investment criteria, the above recommendations are left to the discretion of the AE.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.02/Rev.01  
## Page 195  

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:  

We would like to express our appreciation to the iTAP for their overall remarks on this project and for the recommendations provided.  

#### (a) During implementation under Sub-Activity 2.1.1.4, the Government of Pakistan will develop a proposed plan for future EbA and grey-green infrastructure projects in additional sites. This will include a proposed resource mobilization plan. The Government of Pakistan and its donor partners, such as USAID, have expressed an interest in supporting the replication of these interventions in other sites beyond the Recharge Pakistan project.  

#### (b) Under Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2, the project will establish protocols for assessing the efficiency and effectiveness of EbA and green and green-grey infrastructure interventions on ecosystems and their flood attenuation and water resource-related services. We will also subject the protocols to peer review (ideally through a third-party scientific journal), to ensure their credibility and make them more widely available to the adaptation community, including the GCF.  

#### (c) Additional efforts will be undertaken to take into account other relevant scientific and technical references in the design of the EbA, and green-grey infrastructure interventions. Under the project, a PhD level freshwater scientist will be responsible for ensuring that additional scientific references are consulted.  

#### (d) The project’s ESMF has been updated to include the risk of waterborne diseases associated with the establishment of retention ponds. To mitigate these risks, all project outputs, meaning all activities under a given output, in each landscape will be subject to the project’s ESS screening, which includes a category on health and safety, to identify possible risks and create any necessary mitigation measures or plans.

# Preface

For the project team that has delivered this assessment, it was much more a labor of love than a routine consulting assignment. The core team gave much more time and effort than originally intended, to cover the expanding scope of the work, but did so without complaint because the subject was very close to our hearts. In addition to their vast combined experience in water, environment, and climate change sectors, three of the four members of the core team have been actively involved in the global and Pakistani women’s movement and in women-led research organizations and have been able to bring their vast experience in these sectors to inform and enrich this assessment.

The core team that has envisaged, designed, managed, analyzed and reported on this assessment are the following:

- Ms. Simi Kamal (Team Leader)
- Ms. Meher M. Noshirwani (Gender Specialist)
- Ms. Kausar Hashmi (Field / Data Collection Expert)
- Mr. Farzand Ali (IRS Manager Operations)

The field and logistical team from IRS Global, colleagues from DevCon, government officers from the field sites and WWF staff members have been duly mentioned under the Acknowledgements that follow separately.

We believe that the material in this assessment can be used for a number of purposes. It can directly feed into the Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the proposed GCF project (as has been done in the last chapter of this report). It can also help WWF improve its own systems to enhance gender inclusion and gender leadership, and initiate measures in its programmatic interventions to propel women’s voices, enhance women’s empowerment and institute gender mainstreaming.

# Executive Summary

The World Wildlife Fund (WWF) / Green Climate Fund (GCF) on behalf of and in collaboration with the Government of Pakistan, is preparing a proposal for the project Recharge Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to Climate Change through ecosystem-based adaptation for Integrated Flood Risk Management.

In this context, the WWF Recharge Pakistan project has conducted a Gender Analysis and developed a Gender Action Plan to ensure that gender mainstreaming is integrated into the design phase of the project.

The project’s proposed activities will be implemented under three components:  
• Component 1: Proofs of concept for EbA and green infrastructure interventions as efficient and effective solutions for flood and drought risk reduction in Pakistan.  
• Component 2: Enabling a paradigm shift towards EbA and green infrastructure in Pakistan.  
• Component 3: Enhanced community resilience and adoption of EbA and green infrastructure interventions in Pakistan’s Indus Basin.

As a result of WWF -Pakistan’s engagements during Concept Note and Funding Proposal development with national (MoCC and MoWR), local (provincial irrigation departments) and international (e.g., World Bank, ADB, KfW, GIZ, etc.), the proposed project was designed to have a total of ~ US$67 million in government co-finance from the NDRMF (~US$37 million) and MoWR (~US$29 million) committed to the project to complement the GCF grant. While ~US$67 million in government co-finance from the NDRMF and MoWR was committed to the project to complement the GCF grant, the recent 2022 floods have required that these funds be repurposed for immediate disaster relief making it very difficult for the Government of Pakistan to commit significant adaptation finance at this time. Despite these constraints, the GoP remains committed to the proposed project but is unable to commit any co-financing for the project due to the economic constraints on public spending due to the high costs for recovery from the 2022 floods.

As a result of the above-mentioned co-financing no longer being available for the project, the project has been re-scaled from 6 sites [Manchar, Kaha, Taunsa, Chakar Lehri, DI Khan and Ramak] to four sites [Manchar, Chakar Lehri, DI Khan and Ramak]. This gender assessment was undertaken for all six sites and includes, in full, the gender assessment for the remaining four sites that will be targeted under the project. The Gender Action Plan has been revised to reflect the activities required only in the four project sites to ensure the project interventions are gender-responsive.

The framework for the Gender Analysis was based on an Assessment Grid with 26 questions, outlining the secondary and primary data requirements. The following steps were designed and implemented:  

• Development of Assessment Grid  
• Literature Review and Secondary Data covering the status of women in Pakistan, global and Pakistani GBV and SEAH (gender-based violence including sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment) situation, policies of relevant sectors, examples of relevant projects, women’s participation and leadership in the environment sectors, impact of climate change, and participation and women’s leadership in WWF

# Status of Women in Pakistan

The position and status of women in Pakistan has a checkered history. Over the last three decades the status of women has progressed, remained static, regressed, and constantly changes depending on many structural factors. Numerous laws have been promulgated ensuring women rights and protection, but their implementation and lack of political will renders them largely ineffective. Very little attention is placed on the crucial nexus of culture, religion and policy development and implementation of laws. The plethora of laws, negative socio-norms and practices have become concrete in Pakistan's patriarchal society in a way that they consistently favor men and place women at disadvantage.

Due to some of the above-mentioned reasons, women in Pakistan lack (or have inadequate) capabilities to fully and meaningfully participate in decision making processes, political/governance spaces, and in policies and programs. Formal institutions including both private and public, fall short of facilitating women's access to human, financial and physical capital i.e. education, skills, health facilities, loans and physical assets. Informal institutions (family, community, religion, segregation, marriage) define and affirm norms that give precedence to marriage/child marriage over education and employment, prescribe gendered roles, and condone domestic violence/gender-based violence. Laws have therefore not had the expected impact.

The World Economic Forum Global Gender Gap Report of 2022 found that Pakistan is the second worst country in the world in terms of gender parity; ranking Pakistan 145 out of 146 countries worldwide.

Pakistan is among the countries that are most vulnerable to the risks associated with climate change and ranks 8th on the Global Climate Risk Index 2021.

Pakistan's women are further disadvantaged because 9.1 million women agricultural workers play a substantial role in food production and food security, but they are largely unpaid, suffer from greater time poverty, and are far more vulnerable to exploitation than men. Women's ownership of land, and control over physical assets is minimal: only 2 percentage of women own a house or agricultural land.

Unfortunately, the recent Gender Policy Framework (March 2022) of the Government of Pakistan needs to do more to close the gender gaps and propel the levels and scope of action required to counter anti-women norms and practices. A study conducted by UN highlights that the recent floods of 2022 "serve as a case in point, pushing back many of the gains made by the women in Pakistan in over a decade" due to poor governance, weak response mechanisms and plans that are made without the gender lens.

The experiences of the 2005 earthquake, and the floods of 2010 and 2011 have shown that the patriarchal norms that shield men from their crimes against women, and victimize the women instead, become even stronger in emergencies. The floods of 2022 have once again

1 World Economic Forum, Insight Report: Global Gender Gap Report 2022.  
2 UNDP, Gender Equality in Pakistan: Climatic & Politico-Economic Stressors VOLUME 9 / ISSUE 4 | DECEMBER 2022 - JANUARY 2023

# 10

Exposed women to increased risk of gender-based violence and discrimination which could lead to sexual abuse, harassment, exploitation, and early and forced marriages. The United Nations Population Fund noted that there are almost 650,000 pregnant women in flood-affected areas, of which almost 73,000 were due to give birth in September 2022. These women are particularly vulnerable and face numerous health and safety risks. With the lack of adequate protection and safeguarding mechanisms in flood-affected districts, women and girls will be largely cut off from the support they need.

However, the presence of the National Commission on the Status of Women, Provincial Commissions, the Women’s Caucus in Parliament and NGOs working on women’s rights, gender mainstreaming, combatting violence against women should not be underestimated. The project should make links with these organizations and work with them proactively to develop the support actions needed to support women in the four project sites. Links with local government offices, extension services, law enforcement and justice offices will be key to helping women and men to access government services and obtain redress.

## Gender Based Violence (GBV) and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH)

According to Global Database on violence against women, 24.5% have experienced lifetime physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence between 2017 and 2018. There is no confirmed national level comprehensive statistics on GBV and SEAH. Post 2022 floods in Pakistan, there have been multiple studies and gender needs assessments conducted by multi-lateral donors, but no specific study has been conducted that focuses purely on GBV and SEAH related incidence in the aftermath of this disastrous floods. However, it can be safely said that SEAH is subsumed under the terms Gender Based Violence (GBV), ‘honor killing’ and ‘domestic abuse’. GBV and SEAH are complex issues and are a means of control to maintain power structures, traditional norms, customary laws, and access to resources, all resulting in the oppression of women and gender inequalities.

Another dimension of GBV is the connection with the environment - pressures on the environment and its natural resources increase gender inequalities and power imbalances in communities and households coping with resource scarcity, climate change, and environmental degradation. The linkages between environmental degradation, climate change and the impact of covid-19 has led to an increase in GBV and SEAH, as has been documented globally.

In terms of legislation, Pakistan is signatory to international conventions, and has many progressive and supportive laws that could provide holistic mitigation to and address GBV and SEAH. Unfortunately, other laws that remain on the books (such as the Hudood Ordinances) counter the progressive laws a thus setting back any progress already made. The net result is that women rarely get the justice and redress they need. The effectiveness of these laws in relation to gender issues can be measured according to the Gender Integration Continuum given below. The scale has been coined to measure the level of responsiveness of a particular policy or law to not only identify but also address gender issues specifically climate change, insecurity (GBV and SEAH) and gender nexus.

3 https://pakistan.unfpa.org/en/news/women-and-girls-bearing-brunt-pakistan-monsoon-floods

# [Page 1]

## 11

Based on the Gender Integration scale, the following policies and laws are advantageous and supportive to the project.

The focus group discussions have revealed the existence of various forms of gender-based violence in the field sites. Even though direct questions were not asked about SEAH to community members participating in FGDs (since this would have required a specialized approach using trained psychologists due to the sensitive nature of these questions), physical violence has been mentioned by women respondents as issues they are faced with in their community. Disrespect for women has also been cited which indicates a deeper insidious side of violence which must be addressed if gender equality and women empowerment is to bring real change to the lives of the women in the communities, and their household and country in general.
